Takes a free-text search query (e.g. `python run_custom_audit.py \"NBA\"` or `python run_custom_audit.py \"Zalgiris\"`)\n2. Scans **all historical JSON prediction files** back to the tracking epoch\n3. Filters to only leagues with **≥5 graded games** (valid leagues check)\n4. Groups results by **Stability Tier** (1–4) based on raw volatility thresholds\n5. Shows hit rates at **Flat Floor, -5, -10, -15, -20** buffer windows\n6. Appends **SDI (Star Dependency Index)** data per team if Proballers box scores exist\n\n---\n\n## Is It Useful or Redundant?\n\n### It is **genuinely useful and NOT redundant** — but it uses a **completely different paradigm** than `standard_confidence_audit.py`:\n\n| Dimension | `run_custom_audit.py` | `standard_confidence_audit.py` |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Primary axis** | Search by team/league name | Audit by confidence tier/band |\n<truncated 2664 bytes>"}

{"step_index":5137,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-16T15:45:32Z","content":"Correct — I'd say **no, don't replace them**. Here's the reasoning:\n\n---\n\n## The Volatility Tiers Serve a Different Purpose\n\nThe confidence bands (`[HIGH]`, `[SOLID]`) answer: **\"How predictable is this game overall?\"** — composite of vol + MAE + bias + spread.\n\nThe volatility tiers in `run_custom_audit.py` answer: **\"When this team plays in a stable/volatile matchup, how does the total behave?\"** — pure scoring consistency.\n\nFor pre-match total validation, **both questions are relevant but different**:\n\n- `[HIGH]` tells you the model is trustworthy *right now* for this specific game\n- Tier 1 history tells you this team *historically* plays in low-variance games at -10 = 80% hit rate\n\nThat's complementary, not redundant.\n\n---\n\n## The Real Problem Is Just the Labelling\n\nThe confusion comes from the fact that **Tier 1/2/3/4 sounds like it should map to your confidence bands**, but it doesn't. Someone reading the output could reasonably assume Tier 1 = `[HIGH]` which is wrong.\n\n<truncated 992 bytes>"}

Here's why:\n\n```\nModel total:       215\nBookmaker line:    210.5  ← where you actually bet\n\nYour edge:         4.5 pts above the bookie line\nRelevant buffer:   ~-5 (not -10)\n```\n\nThe buffer you should look at in the audit should roughly **match your edge over the bookmaker line** — not a fixed -10 across all games.\n\n---\n\n## What the Buffers Actually Mean for Betting\n\n| You bet OVER at... | Look at buffer... | You need hit rate of... |\n|---|---|---|\n| Model - 2 pts | Flat floor | ≥ 55% |\n| Model - 5 pts | -5 buffer | ≥ 55% |\n| Model - 10 pts | -10 buffer | ≥ 55% |\n| Model - 15 pts | -15 buffer | ≥ 55% |\n\nThe **minimum breakeven** for a standard -110 bet is **52.4%**. You want to be at **55%+** for meaningful edge, **60%+** for strong edge.\n\n<truncated 1476 bytes>"}

{"step_index":5756,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-16T21:05:10Z","content":"Great question — and it's actually more nuanced for hit rate than it was for Volatility and MAE. Let me reason through all three options:\n\n---\n\n**For Volatility and MAE, Worst Maximum is clearly right** because those measure *risk exposure*. One chaotic team creates variance in the total regardless of the opponent.\n\n**For Hit Rate, the question is different.** We are asking: *\"In games involving this team, does the total go over?\"* Each team's hit rate already implicitly includes their opponents' scoring — it's a game-level stat, not an individual scoring stat. So when A plays B, both signals are informative.\n\n---\n\n| Method | A=85%, B=35% | A=80%, B=70% | Implication |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| **Worst Max (Min)** | 35% | 70% | B is the veto — one bad team kills the bet |\n| **Average** | 60% | 75% | They partially cancel — lenient |\n| **Multiplicative** | 30% | 56% | Pure probability math (most theoretically correct) |\n\n---\n\n### My Recommendation: **Minimum (Worst Maximum)**\n\n<truncated 841 bytes>"}
